[32m│[0m  [32mOutlook[0m                                                                                                                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m2026 market outlook: A multidimensional polarization[0m                                                                                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mGlobal Research[0m                                                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m2026 market outlook: A multidimensional polarization[0m                                                                                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mDecember 09, 2025[0m                                                                                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAgainst a backdrop of uneven monetary policy, relentless AI expansion and intensifying market polarization, what’s the outlook for equities, commodities, currencies[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mand more?[0m                                                                                                                                                             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mOverview[0m                                                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mKey takeaways[0m                                                                                                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mJ.P. Morgan Global Research is positive on global equities for 2026, forecasting double-digit gains across both developed markets (DM) and emerging markets (EM).[0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mLooking at the economy, J.P. Morgan Global Research forecasts a 35% probability of a U.S. and global recession in 2026, and sticky inflation will likely remain a [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mprevailing theme.[0m                                                                                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mMost DM central banks are expected to either stay on hold or conclude their easing cycle in the first half of the year.[0m                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWhile J.P. Morgan Global Research continues to be bearish on the dollar for 2026, it is moderately bullish on the euro.[0m                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“We expect the global economy to remain resilient in 2026, with AI investment continuing to drive market dynamics and support growth.”[0m                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mHussein Malik[0m                                                                                                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mHead of Global Research, J.P. Morgan[0m                                                                                                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m| 00:54[0m                                                                                                                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mMeera Chandan on the 2026 global FX outlook[0m                                                                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWill evolving fiscal and monetary policy provide tailwinds for the FX market?[0m                                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m| 00:54[0m                                                                                                                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mMeera Chandan on the 2026 global FX outlook[0m                                                                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWill evolving fiscal and monetary policy provide tailwinds for the FX market?[0m                                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mView Transcript[0m                                                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mHide Transcript[0m                                                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAudio Descriptive Off[0m                                                                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAudio Descriptive On[0m                                                                                                                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThere are two main macro themes that we’ll be focused on. The first is that central banks are going to be transitioning from what was a simultaneous easing cycle in[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m2025, to a simultaneous hold at relatively high levels above pre-COVID levels in 2026.[0m                                                                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSo, this is a pretty big transition where 70% of central banks were cutting rates, now no longer doing that — relatively inactive. The second pillar of the macro [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mtheme is that this is going to be a fairly pro-cyclical dynamic going on in the background.[0m                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWe’re seeing growth forecasts getting upgraded across the curve as we’re going into the new year. And there’s a lot of fiscal and monetary policy easing that is [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mgoing to provide a tailwind for an improving growth backdrop. So, inactive central banks, strong growth.[0m                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIf it sounds like the middle of the dollar smile and a low-volatility environment, that’s exactly what it is.[0m                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m| 00:58[0m                                                                                                                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBruce Kasman on the 2026 global economic outlook[0m                                                                                                                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWill inflation remain elevated, and what's in store for the labor market?[0m                                                                                             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m| 00:58[0m                                                                                                                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBruce Kasman on the 2026 global economic outlook[0m                                                                                                                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWill inflation remain elevated, and what's in store for the labor market?[0m                                                                                             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mView Transcript[0m                                                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mHide Transcript[0m                                                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAudio Descriptive Off[0m                                                                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAudio Descriptive On[0m                                                                                                                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mI would make two important points about how we see the macro outlook. We see growth solid and importantly begin to see as we move through the first half of the [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32myear, a recoupling of the labor markets, which have been quite weak, to overall economic performance.[0m                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSo the pickup in job growth, a more normal and balanced pattern of growth, is I think the first and most important part of our view. The second part of our view, [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mwhich is important, is we don’t think you can have your cake and eat it too.[0m                                                                                          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSo, with growth being solid, with labor markets starting to show stronger demand, with sentiment picking up, we think the overall environment is going to be one in [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mwhich inflation stays elevated.[0m                                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWhere labor markets in some key countries, including the United States, begin to tighten again and central banks don’t deliver on the expectations that markets have[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mfor interest rate declines.[0m                                                                                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWhat’s the overall outlook for global markets in 2026?[0m                                                                                                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe year ahead will likely be defined by the collision of uneven monetary policy, the relentless expansion of AI and intensifying market polarization. These [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mdrivers, along with the evolving U.S. policy agenda, will continue to reshape the global macro and market landscape.[0m                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“At the heart of our outlook is a multidimensional polarization: equity markets split between AI and non-AI sectors, a U.S. economy balancing robust capex with soft[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mlabor demand, and a widening divide in household spending,” said Dubravko Lakos-Bujas, head of Global Markets Strategy at J.P. Morgan.[0m                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAll things considered, J.P. Morgan Global Research sees a resilient global growth outlook for 2026 thanks to factors including front-loaded fiscal policy support. [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mHowever, downside risks remain elevated, given weak business sentiment and the ongoing slowdown in the labor market. At the same time, the tailwinds of 2025 — such [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mas healthy balance sheets for corporates and households, ample liquidity and the broadening of AI capex spending — will likely persist in 2026, driving earnings [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mexpansion.[0m                                                                                                                                                            [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“Overall, the market environment remains fragile, and investors must navigate a landscape where risk and resilience coexist,” added Fabio Bassi, head of Cross-Asset[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mStrategy at J.P. Morgan.[0m                                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“At the heart of our outlook is a multidimensional polarization: equity markets split between AI and non-AI sectors, a U.S. economy balancing robust capex with soft[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mlabor demand, and a widening divide in household spending.”[0m                                                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mDubravko Lakos-Bujas[0m                                                                                                                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mHead of Global Markets Strategy, J.P. Morgan[0m                                                                                                                          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mEquity markets[0m                                                                                                                                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mJ.P. Morgan Global Research is positive on global equities for 2026, forecasting double-digit gains across both developed markets (DM) and emerging markets (EM). [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThis bullish outlook is buttressed by factors including robust earnings growth, lower rates, declining policy headwinds and the continued rise of AI .[0m                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“The AI-driven supercycle is fueling record capex and rapid earnings expansion. This momentum is spreading geographically and across a diverse list of industries, [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mfrom technology and utilities to banks, healthcare and logistics, creating winners and losers in the process,” Lakos-Bujas observed.[0m                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIndeed, AI could further amplify polarization within an already unhealthy K-shaped economy, and market concentration could reach new highs as a result. “In such an [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32menvironment, broad sentiment measures remain prone to sharp swings, even though underlying trends remain intact and fundamentals solid,” Lakos-Bujas said.[0m            [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mRegional outlooks[0m                                                                                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mUS[0m                                                                                                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIn 2026, style positioning will likely resemble 2025, with new extremes in crowding, record concentration and a “winner-takes-all” dynamic. Looking at the S&P 500, [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mJ.P. Morgan Global Research estimates the AI supercycle driving above-trend earnings growth of 13–15% for at least the next two years.[0m                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mEurozone[0m                                                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mActivity momentum in the eurozone is likely to improve in 2026, thanks to better credit impulse and the rollout of fiscal stimulus. Earnings are expected to grow by[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m13%+ next year, supported by stronger operating leverage, reduced tariff headwinds, easier comps and better financing conditions.[0m                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mJapan[0m                                                                                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSanaenomics — the economic policies of new Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi — and corporate reforms will likely propel Japanese equities in 2026. Businesses [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mwill likely focus on unlocking excess cash, which could in turn fuel capital investment, wage growth and shareholder returns. In addition, Sanaenomics is expected [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mto revive middle-class spending and strategic investments, providing tailwinds for the market.[0m                                                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mEM[0m                                                                                                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mEM equities are positioned for robust performance in 2026, boosted by lower local interest rates, higher earnings growth, attractive valuations, ongoing [0m             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mimprovements in corporate governance, healthier fiscal balance sheets and resilient global growth. China could see green shoots emerging in the private sector after[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32ma multi-year slowdown, while Korea remains supported by governance reforms and AI. Elsewhere, LatAm could experience strong upside thanks to outsized monetary [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mpolicy stimulus and key political shifts.[0m                                                                                                                             [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mThe global economy[0m                                                                                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe global expansion is at an important juncture. While GDP growth has been resilient through 2025, imbalances have formed as demand has rotated toward tech capex [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mand job gains have stalled.[0m                                                                                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“Business caution is the primary drag on hiring, reflecting trade war concerns and sluggish non-tech demand. In turn, weak labor demand is starting to erode [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mpurchasing power, particularly in the U.S., where softening private sector labor income growth is combining with firming inflation and a concentrated near-term [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mpublic sector drag,” said Bruce Kasman, chief global economist at J.P. Morgan.[0m                                                                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mTo this end, J.P. Morgan Global Research sees consumption downshifting in DM in the fourth quarter of 2025, with a 35% probability of a U.S. and global recession in[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m2026. However, global GDP growth is expected to receive a boost in the first half of the year thanks to front-loaded fiscal stimulus, promoting a rebound in [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32msentiment.[0m                                                                                                                                                            [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“Our baseline forecast sees the health of the business sector, supportive financial conditions and fiscal stimulus as the ingredients that will enable the global [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32meconomy to absorb the sentiment shock currently depressing labor demand. If we are right, job growth and sentiment will pick up as we move through the first half of[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m2026, supporting the recoupling of labor demand with solid GDP gains,” Kasman noted. In addition, an AI spending wave could deliver a limited boost to the global [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32meconomy.[0m                                                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSticky inflation will likely remain a prevailing theme. After unwinding supply shocks related to the pandemic and the Russia-Ukraine war, inflation has hovered [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32maround 3% with little sign of moving lower. “Upward pressure on global goods prices related to the trade war is likely to be transitory, but we expect elevated [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mgoods price pressures to remain in place, at least through the first half of 2026,” Kasman added.[0m                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mGlobal outlook scenarios[0m                                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mView text Version[0m                                                                                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mJ.P. Morgan Global Research sees a 35% probability of a U.S. and global recession in 2026.[0m                                                                            [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mView Infographic[0m                                                                                                                                                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mView text Version[0m                                                                                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mRates[0m                                                                                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mJ.P. Morgan Global Research assumes growth will run at or above potential across most DMs in 2026, and that inflation will continue declining — albeit remaining [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32msticky in several jurisdictions. This could create a further decoupling in monetary policy outcomes: for instance, the Federal Reserve (Fed) is expected to cut [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mrates by another 50 basis points (bp), while the Bank of Japan (BoJ) is expected to hike by the same amount. Elsewhere, most DM central banks will likely either [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mstay on hold or conclude their easing cycle in the first half of the year.[0m                                                                                            [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mHowever, risks surrounding this baseline view remain. In the U.S., downside risks from a more persistent cyclical weakening in the labor market contrast with upside[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mrisks to growth stemming from AI adoption — both of which could impact the Fed’s reaction function in different ways. And in the U.K., there is potential for [0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mfurther bouts of increased term premia around fiscal events, with political uncertainty also increasing.[0m                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mOverall, DM yields are forecast to grind higher over the course of 2026. 10-year Treasuries, 10-year Bunds and 10-year gilts could reach 4.35%, 2.75% and 4.75% [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mrespectively by the fourth quarter, with mixed curve performance. “We project Treasury yields will remain range-bound over the next few months, before rebounding [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mmoderately once the Fed goes on hold in the spring,” said Jay Barry, head of Global Rates Strategy at J.P. Morgan. “Outside of the U.S., we see Bunds and gilts [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mmarkets maintaining the same ranges they occupied in 2025, and potentially getting pulled cheaper within them as U.S. yields start to trend higher into the middle [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mof the year.”[0m                                                                                                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIn Asia, J.P. Morgan Global Research remains bearish on Japanese government bonds (JGBs) and expects broad bear-flattening dynamic. “We do not yet see any clear [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mevidence to suggest a bullish trend reversal looks imminent, especially as other DM markets look set to potentially weaken into the middle of next year,” Barry [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32madded.[0m                                                                                                                                                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFX[0m                                                                                                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mJ.P. Morgan Global Research continues to be bearish on the dollar in the coming year. “Our dollar view for 2026 is net bearish, albeit smaller in magnitude and less[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32muniform in breadth than in 2025,” said Meera Chandan, co-head of Global FX Strategy at J.P. Morgan. “The combination of a Fed that continues to fret about labor [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mmarket softness and a middle-of-the-smile risk environment that supports the high-yield FX cohort should deliver a lower dollar on the whole, but the runway for [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mweakness is constrained by solid U.S. growth and sticky inflation.”[0m                                                                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mOn the other hand, J.P. Morgan Global Research is moderately bullish on the euro — a view supported by eurozone growth and German fiscal expansion. “However, any [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mEUR/USD gains should be moderate versus 2025 unless U.S. data materially weakens,” Chandan said.[0m                                                                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mElsewhere, there could be a buy-the-dip opportunity in sterling given resilient domestic growth, an anticipated global growth upswing and a carry-friendly [0m           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32menvironment. “The structural drags for GBP are not going away, and this is why we’re taking more of a tactical buy-the-dip approach rather than turning more [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mstrategically bullish,” explained James Nelligan, an FX strategist at J.P. Morgan. “In our view, strength in sterling is more likely to come in the first half of [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthe year, with the second half seeing fiscal fears coming back into focus ahead of the next budget, meaning underperformance becomes more of a central risk.”[0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIn Japan, the sharp rise in USD/JPY has come to a halt, but the yen still declined slightly in 2025 — highlighting the challenge for it to sustainably outperform [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mwhile interest rates remain negative. “In 2026, as the easing cycle among G-10 central banks nears its end, it will become even more difficult to prevent JPY [0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mdepreciation through policy measures such as rate hikes or intervention,” said Junya Tanase, chief Japan FX strategist at J.P. Morgan. “Plus, if the initial budget [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mfor FY26 confirms the Takaichi administration’s expansionary fiscal stance, growing concerns about fiscal sustainability could heighten downward pressure on JPY.”[0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mForecasts for major currency pairs[0m                                                                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mView text Version[0m                                                                                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBy December 2026, EUR/USD is expected to reach 1.20, GBP/USD 1.36 and USD/JPY 164.[0m                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mView Infographic[0m                                                                                                                                                      [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mCredit[0m                                                                                                                                                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAcross global credit markets, focus is shifting from the macro to the micro, and spreads are expected to widen in 2026. “Capex-related issuance across the AI and [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAI-adjacent ecosystems will take their toll on high-grade spreads, in particular. Plus, M&A and increased leveraged buyout activity will be a factor driving [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mincreased issuance across both high-grade and high-yield,” said Stephen Dulake, co-head of Global Fundamental Research at J.P. Morgan.[0m                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThis dynamic is especially evident in the U.S. high-grade space. “While in recent years we have been more anxious about the macro backdrop disrupting spreads via [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthe channel of lower yields, next year we are forecasting a relatively benign macro environment,” said Nathaniel Rosenbaum, a strategist on the U.S. High-Grade [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mCredit team at J.P. Morgan. “What concerns us more is that high-grade corporates appear to be leaning into this constructive backdrop to increase leverage. With [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32myields 35 bp lower than the starting point for 2025 and 130 bp below the cycle highs, funding costs are no longer as much of an impediment to capex and M&A [0m          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mactivity.”[0m                                                                                                                                                            [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mTo this end, J.P. Morgan Global Research expects to see moderately wider spreads of 110 bp in U.S. high-grade by year-end 2026. “Even with this widening, high-grade[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mbond spreads will remain quite tight by historical standards. This implies a flat excess return and a total return of 3.0%,” Rosenbaum added.[0m                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIn Europe, the credit cycle is likely to advance in an environment of cheaper funding rates and reduced policy uncertainty, with some corporates releveraging their [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mbalance sheets. Furthermore, global data center construction and European infrastructure spending could require significant amounts of funding from both public and [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mprivate credit markets.[0m                                                                                                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“Investors in European investment-grade credit markets are suffering from recession fatigue after a string of scares in recent years, making them reluctant to price[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mforward economic risk. Consequently, we are likely to stay in the current tight spread environment until we experience an actual downturn, with our forecast seeing [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mspreads essentially flat at 90 bp,” said Daniel Lamy, head of European Credit Strategy at J.P. Morgan.[0m                                                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFor European high-yield, default rates are expected to remain in the 3–4% range for the third consecutive year, but elevated recoveries are limiting credit losses. [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“We look for the current tight spread regime to continue, with a 2026 forecast of 300 bp, equivalent to a 5.5% total return,” Lamy added.[0m                             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mEmerging markets[0m                                                                                                                                                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mLower macro volatility looks set to support EM local markets in 2026. Overall growth (excluding China) is forecast to maintain a trend-like pace of 3.3%, helped by [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mfactors including fading tail-risks on tariffs, easier monetary policy and ongoing tech capex. This resilience largely reflects EM export strength, with limited [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mspillover to domestic demand.[0m                                                                                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIn China , growth should outperform in the first half of the year — especially the first quarter — thanks to the delayed execution of supplementary fiscal measures [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mand the usual front-loading of budget spending. “How much growth slows in the second half will depend on whether — and how quickly — the government introduces [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32madditional policy support,” said Tingting Ge, an economist at J.P. Morgan.[0m                                                                                            [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mEM headline inflation (excluding China and Turkey) is expected to stabilize close to target at 3.2% for 2026. However, while EM core inflation has, on average, [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mreturned to central bank targets, there remains considerable variation across economies. “We continue to see further EM rate cuts in the pipeline, but the pace and [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mbreadth of cuts are expected to moderate in 2026. EM high-yielders are expected to deliver the bulk of our projected EM easing, with easing space more limited in [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthe low-yielders,” said Jahangir Aziz, head of EM Economics Research at J.P. Morgan.[0m                                                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mRisks to this outlook include worryingly high inflation in the U.S., which would cause the Fed to stay on hold. “This is likely to hit EM FX, rates markets and EM [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcredit too, with sell-offs and negative returns resulting,” noted Luis Oganes, head of Global Macro Research at J.P. Morgan. “In addition, a collapse in U.S. equity[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mprices would have knock-on effects for all risk markets, with credit spreads widening. EM would be hit even if the direct impact channels are limited to start [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mwith.”[0m                                                                                                                                                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“We continue to see further EM rate cuts in the pipeline, but the pace and breadth of cuts are expected to moderate in 2026.”[0m                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mJahangir Aziz[0m                                                                                                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mHead of EM Economics Research, J.P. Morgan[0m                                                                                                                            [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mCommodities[0m                                                                                                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWorld oil demand is expanding, with expected growth of 0.9 million barrels per day (mbd) in 2026 and 1.2 mbd in 2027. Yet, supply is forecast to outstrip demand by [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthreefold in 2026, before moderating to roughly one-third of that pace in 2027 — creating a significant surplus, at least on paper.[0m                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mHowever, these market imbalances are unlikely to fully materialize in practice, as adjustments are expected on both the supply and demand sides. “We expect the [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mmarket will find equilibrium through a combination of rising demand — driven by lower prices — and a mix of voluntary and involuntary production cuts. In line with [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthis outlook, we maintain our Brent price forecast of $58 in 2026 and introduce our 2027 forecast of $57, while recognizing that considerable effort will be [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mrequired to stabilize prices at these levels,” said Natasha Kaneva, head of Global Commodities Strategy at J.P. Morgan.[0m                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mElsewhere, rising supply of liquefied natural gas (LNG) looks set to support lower global natural gas prices. “As new supply projects come online, we anticipate [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mlonger-term prices will trend downward from current levels. We forecast TTF prices to average 28.75 EUR/MWh in 2026 and 24.75 EUR/MWh in 2027, or 3–4 EUR/MWh below [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcurrent forward prices,” said Otar Dgebuadze, part of the Global Commodities Research team at J.P. Morgan.[0m                                                            [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIn precious metals, J.P. Morgan Global Research remains bullish on gold on the back of boosted central bank buying and robust investor demand. Gold prices are [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mexpected to soar to $5,000/oz by the fourth quarter of 2026, averaging $4,753/oz for the full year. “Silver prices are forecast to rise toward $58/oz by the fourth [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mquarter, averaging $56/oz for the full year, while platinum could continue to trade higher for longer next year before the gears of supply rebalancing gain [0m          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mmomentum,” added Gregory Shearer, head of Base and Precious Metals Strategy at J.P. Morgan.[0m                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFinally, in agriculture markets, implied volatility has ticked up over recent weeks. “While there are no imminent signs of shortage or supply-side stress — outside [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mof the livestock sector and, to a degree, cocoa markets — over the coming seasons, our projections of world agriculture stocks-to-use ratios remain close to [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mmulti-year lows for 2026/27 and 2027/28. This declining availability base, driven by low producer margins, raises sensitivity and price volatility to supply-side [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mdisruptions,” said Tracey Allen, an agricultural commodities strategist at J.P. Morgan.[0m                                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mGlobal commodity price forecasts[0m                                                                                                                                      [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mIn 2026, Brent crude is expected to average $58/bbl, natural gas 28.75 EUR/MWh, gold $4,753/oz, silver $56/oz and wheat $5.65/bu.[0m                                     [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mThe year ahead will likely be driven by three powerful forces: uneven monetary policy, the relentless AI cycle and deepening polarization across markets and [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32meconomies. What are the key opportunities and risks to watch across equities, commodities, currencies and more? Join analysts from J.P. Morgan Global Research as [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthey explore the outlook for 2026. This episode was recorded between December 9 and December 17, 2025.[0m                                                                [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mMislav Matejka: We see a constructive outlook across most markets, with both DM and EM equities expected to beat other assets, in particular cash and bonds…[0m          [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mthat is going to provide a tailwind for an improving growth backdrop.[0m                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBruce Kasman: We do believe sticky inflation overall remains a theme on the global stage, and it is, I think, an important in terms of limiting the ability of [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcentral banks overall to lower interest rates.[0m                                                                                                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSam Azzarello: Welcome to J.P. Morgan's Making Sense. I'm Sam Azzarello, and I lead content strategy for global research here at J.P. Morgan. Today, I'm joined by a[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mfew of my colleagues to discuss our outlook for 2026. To kick things off, we'll hear from Bruce Kasman, our chief global economist. Bruce, thanks so much for being [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mhere today.[0m                                                                                                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBruce Kasman: Thanks, Sam, for having me.[0m                                                                                                                             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSam Azzarello: So Bruce, what surprised you most about the global economy in 2025?[0m                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBruce Kasman: I think the biggest surprise was the resilience of the global economy in the face of some significant negative shocks. The particularly big one was [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthe rise in U.S. tariffs, the trade war that had opened up. But at the same time, I think the resilience of the global economy has not come with balanced growth, [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mand there was another surprise, which was the divergence in a global economy which seemed propelled by strong, uh, CapEx in particularly tech spending in 25, and at[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthe same time, had a fairly sharp slowing in job growth. That juxtaposition of CapEx accelerating in job growth stalling for the most part, uh, was a major [0m          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32msurprise.[0m                                                                                                                                                             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSam Azzarello: Fantastic. Thanks for that, Bruce. Where do you think the global economy is headed in 2026 and what are the key factors to watch?[0m                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBruce Kasman: So the global economy's movement into 2026 to us is being driven by two broad factors. One is the sense that the sentiment shock that came this year [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32min 2025 alongside the trade war begins to fade. And we think that will be an important catalyst for recoupling the labor market to the overall picture on growth. [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mLabor markets have been quite weak. Job growth has nearly stalled across the major economies. And if we're right, we get a lift from fading of some of the concerns [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mabout policy, a lift that's partly supported by the easing we've seen in monetary and perspective easing in fiscal policies. And we therefore get as we move through[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m2026, an alignment of solid growth with more normal performance in terms of job growth rebounding. That's the first thing I would emphasize. The second thing we [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mwould look for is less synchronized, but still overall sticky inflation in that environment. That's partly driven by the continued overall environment of goods [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mprice pressures being different than that they were before the pandemic. And it's also being emphasized by the fact that as job growth recovers here, we continue to[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32msee weak labor supply dynamics, immigration policies, having shifted materially across most advanced economies. And with that, we begin to start to see a turn again[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mtowards labor market tightening, which basically shifts from what has been some softening we've seen in the U.S. and other countries this year. So sticky inflation,[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mweak supply sides due to immigration policy and solid growth as you recouple the labor markets to an overall decent performance we're expecting across most of the [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mglobe. One of the consequences of this, of course, is that central banks do not get the opportunity to validate market expectations for interest rate declines, and [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mwe are not looking for much, if any, additional easing for most central banks, with the exception of a high yielding EM central banks that probably still have more [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mto come.[0m                                                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSam Azzarello: Final question for you. Will sticky inflation remain a prevailing theme next year?[0m                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBruce Kasman: We do believe sticky inflation overall remains a theme on the global stage, and it is, I think, an important in terms of limiting the ability of [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcentral banks overall to lower interest rates. Uh, what I would also suggest, however, is we're moving away from a period in which inflation dynamics are more [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32msynchronized. That was clearly a factor in the post-COVID Russian invasion environment in which these global shocks were so important. Inflation dynamics are going [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mto be more driven by local conditions, particularly labor market tightness. We do expect overall here to be relatively sticky and stable inflation, but also expect [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mto see more variation with perhaps the most notable variation is that we have inflation in Western Europe coming down to target and inflation in the U.S. staying [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mclose to 3%.[0m                                                                                                                                                          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSam Azzarello: Bruce, thanks for your insights. Next up, we have Mislav Matejka, head of global equity strategy. Mislav, thanks so much for joining us today.[0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mMislav Matejka : Hi, Sam. Thanks for having me.[0m                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSam Azzarello: So why don't you start by walking us through the overall outlook for global equities in 2026 across both developed markets and emerging markets.[0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mMislav Matejka: We see a constructive outlook across most markets, uh, with both DM and EM equities expected to beat other assets, in particular cash and bonds, [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mwith equities offering in the range of 10 to 25% upside in our view. And the Bullish goal is driven by the supportive growth policy trade-off where on one hand, we [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mlook for robust earnings delivery next year, and that is underpinned by resilient activity momentum in the U.S., but also an improvement among laggard regions. So [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mChina here, it could show green shoots of consumer recovery, and Euros on fiscal stimulus is likely to become much more impactful. And on the other side, inflation [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mis likely to stay on the downtrend due to subdued brand, slowing wage growth and slowing services inflation, which in turn will keep Fed in particular dovish [0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthrough the first half of 26. And the bond yields therefore are unlikely to break out higher, and especially not to break out higher for the, for the wrong reasons.[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mNow, more specifically for the U.S., we are looking for the rally that is supported by continued rapid AI rollout, and therefore in the U.S., we keep preferences in[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mterms of the styles for large caps and for the growth. In the rest of DM, we think that Eurozone will show better earnings delivery after three years of stalling, [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mand that is on stronger activity and less headwinds from trade and from the FX. Japan was our key DM overweight call for 25, and we still think that it goes up [0m      [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mIndia, and on Brazil.[0m                                                                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSam Azzarello: All right. Certainly a lot for us to keep our eyes on. How is the relentless expansion of AI fueling record CapEx and earnings expansion, and how [0m     [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mMislav Matejka: AI rollout remains a clear positive for markets, uh, focused on the U.S., given that almost half of S&P 500 weight is AI related these days. Now, [0m    [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mSam Azzarello: Next, we'll look at rates with Francis Diamond, head of European Rate Strategy. Francis, thanks for being on the podcast.[0m                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFrancis Diamond: Hi, Sam. Thanks for having me.[0m                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSam Azzarello: What's the overall outlook for rates markets in 2026? And are there any regions you'll be keeping a close eye on?[0m                                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFrancis Diamond: Yeah, well, I think, Sam, there are a variety of different themes for global rates markets for next year, based on our views around monetary policy[0m  [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mbackdrop with some increased divergence, that kind of sets a scene in terms of probably limiting the potential for yields to rally. But I do think that means as a [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mtheme, we can look to see carry opportunities in certain markets where central banks are in hold, particularly in the Euro area and in Sweden. I think there is a [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mbit of divergence across curves, which we'll look to investigate, particularly at the front end of the U.S. curve where rates look probably a little bit too low in [0m  [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mkick in towards the backend of 2026. So I think this, this probably means there's a bit of a theme of cross-market divergence between U.S. and Euro, which we'll be [0m  [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mintermediate yields from the second quarter of next year, and also the fiscal backdrop and issuance dynamics will be a focus for Japanese rate markets as well.[0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSam Azzarello: Very interesting insights. Do you see monetary policy continuing to diverge across the globe next year?[0m                                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFrancis Diamond: Yes, for sure. I think there's definitely scope for some divergence across the DM central banks. It's probably limited in nature, but certainly [0m     [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mall on hold over 2026. And I think it's only the Fed and BOE are expected to ease a little more. We forecast one further 25 basis point cut from the Fed in January,[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mwhich is a bit less than what's priced into the front end of the curve in terms of the terminal rate over 2026. So we see terminal rate getting close to, or getting[0m  [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32myields are grinding a little bit lower, to be honest, in terms of a modest rally over the next few months. And if we look further out, we see 10-year bonds, [0m         [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mSam Azzarello: Now, let's hear from Meera Chandan, co-head of Global FX Strategy. Hi, Meera. Great to have you on the podcast. Looking ahead, what are the main [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mforces shaping the FX market in 2026?[0m                                                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mMeera Chandan: There are two main macro themes that we'd be focused on. The first is that central banks are going to be transitioning from what was a simultaneous [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32measing cycle, in 2025 to a simultaneous hold at relatively high levels above pre-COVID levels in 2026. So this is a pretty big transition where 70% of central banks[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mwere cutting rates, now no longer doing that, relatively inactive. The second pillar of the macro theme is that this is going to be a fairly pro-cyclical dynamic [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mgoing on in the background. We're seeing growth forecasts getting upgraded across the curve as we going into the new year, and there's a lot of fiscal and monetary [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mpolicy easing, that is going to provide a tailwind for an improving growth backdrop. So inactive central banks, strong growth. If it sounds like the middle of the [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mdollar smile, and a low volatility environment, that's exactly what it is.[0m                                                                                            [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mrates unchanged for a long period of time, or they're going to cut a lot, either for data-driven reasons, or perhaps if political pressures start to mount. But [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mregardless, I think what that means is any kind of dollar strength we get is going to be quite bounded, whereas the dollar weakness could be more open on the [0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mdownside. I think there could be periods, long periods of dollar consolidating, at relatively unchanged levels because if U.S. growth is 2% and is yielding 3.5%, [0m    [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mquickly. This is going to put pressure on risky asset, especially those that have benefit from easy monetary policy. Finally, we have to talk about AI skepticism. [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWith valuation running hot and a lot of hype in the market, there is a risk that investors start to question the sustainability of the AI boom. While we believe [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthat fundamental, CapEx, sales growth, earnings, and buybacks will support the AI sector, we can't rule out episodes of volatility or temporary retracement. In [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mshort, 2026 will be a year where investor needs to stay agile. Keep an eye on both the upside and the downside, and be ready to pivot as the data and the headline [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mevolves.[0m                                                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mNatasha Kaneva: Fabio, thanks for sharing the cross-asset view.[0m                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFabio Bassi: Great chatting with you.[0m                                                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSam Azzarello: All right. That wraps up our 2026 outlook episode, here on Making Sense. We hope you found the insights from our analysts helpful and insightful, and[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mwe wanna thank you for tuning in. For more market insights, be sure to visit jpmorgan.com/research .[0m                                                                  [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mResearch[0m                                                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFour Key Investment Themes for 2026[0m                                                                                                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mJan 27, 2026[0m                                                                                                                                                          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mMorgan Stanley Research’s key investment themes for this year are Tech Diffusion, The Future of Energy, The Multipolar World and Societal Shifts.[0m                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mKey Takeaways[0m                                                                                                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIn 2025, Morgan Stanley’s thematic stock categories rose 38% and outperformed both the S&P 500 and the MSCI World.[0m                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mCompanies’ and governments’ adaptation to the dynamics of a multipolar world is likely to accelerate this year.[0m                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAI adoption should be a critical driver of stock performance in 2026.[0m                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe Future of Energy theme now has a stronger political element as higher electricity bills could influence voters in different parts of the world.[0m                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSocietal Shifts include the impacts of AI on the job market, longevity, demographic challenges and shifting consumer preferences.[0m                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mEvery January, Morgan Stanley Research identifies the themes that represent the most compelling investment opportunities for the year ahead. This annual list is a [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mresult of deep discussions with macro and industry analysts to understand major trends across sectors and regions.[0m                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFor 2026, the key themes are:[0m                                                                                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAI/Technology Diffusion[0m                                                                                                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe Future of Energy[0m                                                                                                                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe Multipolar World[0m                                                                                                                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSocietal Shifts[0m                                                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe first three themes carry over from last year as the investment opportunities endure, while  Societal Shifts  expands last year’s  Longevity  theme to capture [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAI-driven labor impacts as well as demographic and consumer-behavior transitions.[0m                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThematic investing has proven to be a powerful driver of alpha, or outperformance relative to benchmarks. In 2025, Morgan Stanley’s thematic stock categories gained[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m38% on average, outperforming the MSCI World by 16% and the S&P 500 by 27%.[0m                                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“Thematic analysis has long been central to how we think about markets, especially in periods of extreme volatility,” says Stephen Byrd, Morgan Stanley’s Global [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mHead of Thematic and Sustainability Research. “A thematic lens helps us step back from the noise and focus on the structural forces reshaping economies, industries [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mand societies.”[0m                                                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBelow is a closer look at the 2026 themes and the categories of stocks that investors should take into consideration for each.[0m                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m1[0m                                                                                                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe Multipolar World[0m                                                                                                                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThis was the top-performing thematic category in 2025. The three top-performing thematic categories in 2025 fell within the Multipolar World theme, demonstrating [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mhow geopolitical and industrial shifts are directly influencing markets. Governments continue adopting policies that go against pre-COVID-10  globalization trends ,[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mwhile companies and policymakers are prioritizing control  of supply chains , access to energy, materials, defense capabilities and technology.[0m                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“Policymakers are keen to promote their visions of national and economic security through less open commerce and more local control of supply chains and key [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mtechnologies,” Byrd says. “Multinationals and sovereigns will adapt to this reality in an accelerating pace in 2026, with some facing tougher choices than others.”[0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mStock Categories Within This Theme:  Critical minerals, defense spending and  tech localization .[0m                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m2[0m                                                                                                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAI/Technology Diffusion:[0m                                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAI capabilities should continue to expand at a non-linear pace, potentially faster than the ability of businesses, consumers and governments to adopt them. [0m          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mDiffering adoption curves may become a defining driver of stock performance in 2026.[0m                                                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“This theme has clearly matured,” Byrd says. “A critical evolution is our view that compute demand is likely to exceed supply, even as software and hardware become [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mmore efficient.”[0m                                                                                                                                                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mStock Categories Within This Theme:   AI enablers ,  AI adopters ,  the Humanoid 100  and AI infrastructure.[0m                                                          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m3[0m                                                                                                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe Future of Energy:[0m                                                                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAI infrastructure—particularly data centers— is driving a rapid surge in global energy demand. In the U.S. alone, energy consumption could rise 10% per year over [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthe next decade. Alternative sources of energy and new technologies are being developed to provide low-cost power supply, including carbon capture, energy storage [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mand nuclear power. At the same time, there’s an increased focus on how higher electricity bills could affect consumers in many parts of the world, potentially [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mhaving an impact on how they vote.[0m                                                                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“Compared with 2025, this theme has expanded from a supply conversation into a political one,” Byrd says. “Rising energy costs are becoming increasingly visible to [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mconsumers, elevating the `politics of energy’.”[0m                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mStock Categories Within This Theme:  Powering AI,  the globalization of natural gas ,  the nuclear renaissance , Europe's "low decarb diet," power grid growth & [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mevolution, clean energy & storage, energy security and carbon as a commodity.[0m                                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m4[0m                                                                                                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSocietal Shifts:[0m                                                                                                                                                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAn evolution of 2025’s Longevity theme, Societal Shifts captures the demographic, technological and social changes reshaping economies worldwide. These include the [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32minfluence of  AI on the labor market , aging populations, evolving consumer tastes and the growing divide represented by the “Kshaped economy,” in which [0m             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mhigherincome groups advance while lowerincome groups fall behind.[0m                                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“These shifts increasingly influence government policy, corporate strategy and economic growth—while their impact spans far more industries than investors often [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mexpect,” Byrd says.[0m                                                                                                                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mStock Categories Within This Theme:   Diabesity ecosystem , AI & healthcare, preparing for an aging population, Japan’s longevity, smart chemo,  the future of food [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m& beverages  and addressing AI-driven employment impacts.[0m                                                                                                             [32m│[0m
