[32m│[0m  [32m台灣 United Kingdom United States Location not listed BlackRock iShares Aladdin Our company RESET 1[0m                                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mMarket Insights The Odds Are Changing: 2026 Is a Market for Investors, Not Gamblers Jan 02, 2026 | By Rick Rieder[0m                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIf the last few years felt a bit like walking through a casino where almost every table was paying out, 2025 has been the point in the night when the lights come up[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32ma bit and you notice the house edge again.[0m                                                                                                                            [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFrom 2020 through 2024, the equity market rewarded almost any risk you took. In that period, more than half of S&P 500 companies delivered annualized returns above [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m15%, and about 90% had positive annualized returns. In other words, simply “putting chips on the table” worked unusually well.[0m                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThis year has been different. As 2025 winds down, around 40% of the S&P is heading for a negative year.[0m                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThat shift in the odds is the core of how we’re thinking about 2026. The coming year looks less like a casino and more like an investor’s market. You win not by [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mchasing every hot trade, but by sizing positions thoughtfully and focusing on high-probability outcomes.[0m                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mInflation’s Storm Has Passed; Labor Is the New Pothole[0m                                                                                                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe good news first: the inflation storm that dominated the last few years looks largely behind us.[0m                                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mShelter inflation, which had been a source of stickiness, has moderated back toward pre-COVID trends when you look at six and three-month measures. Underlying price[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mvolatility tells a similar story: the standard deviation of month-over-month core inflation is back in line with the remarkably stable 1990–2020 period.[0m              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mTariffs added noise, but even there the data suggests a one-time level shock. The data shows roughly 0.5 percentage points of tariff pass-through already behind us [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32min core PCE inflation, and perhaps another 0.4 points to come—important, but not grounds for a new inflation spiral. Markets seem to agree, five-year inflation [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mbreak evens are hovering around 2.3%, a level that would have looked almost ideal to policymakers in many prior cycles.[0m                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBureau of Labor Statistics (BLS), as of 09/30/2025[0m                                                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIf inflation is no longer the central problem, what is? Labor.[0m                                                                                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBehind the headline numbers, virtually every measure of underlying slack is moving the wrong way. The share of workers who are “marginally attached” to the labor [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mforce or not in the labor force but wanting a job, has been drifting higher. Layoff announcements show that roughly 70% of October’s job cuts were framed as [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mefficiency initiatives—cost-cutting, automation, restructuring—rather than classic cyclical weakness.[0m                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAt the same time, healthcare has done almost all of the heavy lifting for job creation. The three-month moving average of total job growth excluding healthcare is [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mnow negative for the first time outside of recession in more than 25 years. Put differently, the rest of the economy is no longer creating net new jobs, even as the[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mheadline payroll numbers still look respectable. That support is also beginning to fade, as hiring in healthcare slows toward more normal run-rates.[0m                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe October and November jobs reports reinforce this picture. Hiring has clearly downshifted, and the underemployment rate has risen to 8.7%, the steepest increase [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32msince the pandemic. Wage growth is also rolling over toward the mid-3% range year-on-year. This combination of slower hiring, a clear increase in slack, and [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mmoderating wages is not the backdrop for keeping policy restrictive.[0m                                                                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAn equally important part of the story is affordability. Even with inflation cooling, many key prices (housing, transport, insurance, basic services) have reset [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mhigher and stayed there. Higher rates have also made homeownership less accessible. The result is a more uneven distribution of wealth and consumption. [0m              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mHigher-income households can mostly absorb the shock, while lower and middle-income households are feeling a much tighter squeeze.[0m                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIt’s no surprise, then, that household surveys show expectations for the job market over the next year at some of the worst levels on record.[0m                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBLS, as of 11/30/2025[0m                                                                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mTaken together, this is why we’ve been arguing that the Fed’s challenge in 2026 is no longer about taming inflation - it’s about avoiding unnecessary damage to the [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mparts of the economy that are already under pressure. With the inflation storm largely behind us and labor potholes ahead, we reiterate the need to move away from [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mrestrictive policy toward a more balanced stance.[0m                                                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAI: A Cost Revolution More Than a Revenue Story[0m                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mOne of the reasons the labor backdrop feels so different is that technology is increasingly being deployed to reduce headcount and raise productivity, not just to [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mbuild new products.[0m                                                                                                                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mCorporate commentary this year has been remarkably consistent. Across industries, management teams are talking about “being more efficient,” “reducing bureaucracy,”[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“removing layers,” and “shifting resources” toward their “biggest bets.” The language varies by company, but the theme is the same. Automation and AI are central [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mlevers in cost management and earnings growth.[0m                                                                                                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe long-term implications of this are profound. Today, labor accounts for roughly 55% of total business-sector costs. If AI and related technologies can reduce [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mlabor’s share of corporate costs by even 5%— from 55% to 50%—and if 75% of those savings accrue to corporates and 25% to AI service providers, the present value of [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthose cash flows is enormous. On aggregate, that would generate roughly $1.2 trillion in annual labor cost savings, translating into about $878 billion in [0m           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mincremental after-tax corporate profits each year. The present value of the corporate piece alone is on the order of $82 trillion, with another $27 trillion [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32maccruing to AI providers.[0m                                                                                                                                             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFor perspective, that $110 trillion combined present value doesn’t come from new revenue lines. It comes from shifting cost structures, with labor as the obvious [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mloser. Mechanically, a 9% reduction in labor costs translates into roughly 31% higher earnings for corporates. The market doesn’t need every sector to experience [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthat magnitude of change for the equity and credit implications to be significant.[0m                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBureau of Economic Analysis, as of 6/30/2025 and BlackRock Analysis, as of 12/15/2025. This is an illustrative example not intended to represent actual or future [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mperformance of any product, market or strategy. Hypothetical results have inherent limitations and do not reflect real-world conditions, trading, or market [0m          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mbehavior.[0m                                                                                                                                                             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThis is why we continue to see AI as, first and foremost, a cost and margin story. Over time, it should support higher returns on equity for the companies that [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mdeploy it well.[0m                                                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFor investors, the key is to own the beneficiaries of that transition on both the equity and credit side - businesses with scalable models, durable cash flows, and [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mclear plans for harnessing AI to lift productivity over time.[0m                                                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m2026: Strong Growth, Weaker Labor, More Dispersion[0m                                                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSo where does that leave the macro regime as we look into 2026?[0m                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mA few points stand out:[0m                                                                                                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mGrowth can remain resilient even as labor softens. Real private domestic final purchases continue to point to an economy growing in the neighborhood of 2% in real [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mterms, boosted by robust investment in intellectual property, software, and the AI-related build-out of data centers and power infrastructure.[0m                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mPolicy is still broadly supportive. Fiscal deficits remain large, liquidity is not scarce, and the Fed should continue moving the rate to neutral, all of which [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mprovide a meaningful cushion against a classic demand-side recession.[0m                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mYet the distribution of outcomes is widening. After a decade-plus of financial repression, with rates anchored at the floor, the return to a higher cost of capital [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mis re-introducing dispersion—across sectors, across balance sheets, and across countries. We expect more frequent idiosyncratic defaults and downgrades, even if the[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32maggregate macro data remains respectable.[0m                                                                                                                             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIn that world, 2026 looks like a year in which both upside surprises and downside accidents become more common. It is, in many ways, the best opportunity we’ve seen[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32msince the Global Financial Crisis to play both sides of the distribution: to own high-quality income and durable growth where you’re being paid for the risk, and to[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mbe selective (and sometimes short) where valuations ignore fragility.[0m                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThat’s fundamentally an investor’s market, not a gambler’s.[0m                                                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mLetting Income and Time Do the Heavy Lifting[0m                                                                                                                          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mEven a healthy market doesn’t move in a straight line. We’ve already lived through several clear “air pockets” since 2023—periods where markets stepped down [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mmeaningfully before recovering—and there will almost certainly be more as the regime shifts from easy money and broad speculative upside toward something more [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mnormal and dispersed.[0m                                                                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFor us, those episodes are a reminder of what investing actually is. In a gambling framework, everything depends on the next spin of the wheel. You either win [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mquickly or you walk away. In an investing framework, what matters is whether you own assets that can keep generating cash flow through those air pockets, and [0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mwhether your time horizon is long enough to let that income and compounding work for you.[0m                                                                             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThat’s why we put so much emphasis on durable yield. When markets wobble, income keeps showing up. Over time, that reinvested income does a lot of the work in [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mpulling a portfolio back toward its longer-term path, even in stormy weather.[0m                                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe key, in our view, is to go into this next phase of the cycle owning cash-flow-generative assets, accepting that there will be air pockets, and relying on income[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mand time—rather than short-term luck—to drive outcomes.[0m                                                                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBloomberg, as of 12/15/2025. US HY = Bloomberg US Corporate High Yield Index, US Agg = Bloomberg US Aggregate Index, US IG = Bloomberg US Corporate Bond Index, S&P [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m= S&P 500 Index, US Tech = S&P 500 Information Technology Index, US Semis = Philadelphia Semiconductor Index.[0m                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWhere We’re Putting Chips to Work[0m                                                                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWith that backdrop, how are we allocating capital as we head into 2026?[0m                                                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mQuality Fixed Income[0m                                                                                                                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mInvestment grade credit does not look especially cheap today, so broad beta exposure is unlikely to be a major source of excess return from here. Where this part of[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthe market remains interesting, in our view, is when you can lock in historically high yields lending to solid balance sheets, and in the opportunity to be [0m          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mtactical. That includes leaning into periods of heavy supply, particularly from large hyperscaler and infrastructure related issuers, when new deals offer [0m           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mconcessions that are not consistently available in the secondary market.[0m                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWithin that high-quality core, we also see a role for mortgages and securitized assets as part of the income sleeve. These markets can provide a meaningful yield [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mpickup over government bonds with strong structural protections and diversification benefits, particularly when investors can be selective about structure and [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcollateral quality.[0m                                                                                                                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBloomberg, as of 12/05/2025. 30Y MBS CC Yield = 30 year mortgage backed security current coupon.[0m                                                                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSelect High Yield and Private Opportunities[0m                                                                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBelow investment grade, we are more selective. A higher cost of capital and a more discriminating market should translate into more defaults and downgrades among [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mweaker issuers.[0m                                                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAgainst that backdrop, we prefer idiosyncratic opportunities—stronger balance sheets, customized protections, and efficient parts of the capital structure tied to [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcash-generative businesses—over broad exposure to the lowest-quality segments of the market.[0m                                                                          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWithin that high-quality core, we also see a role for mortgages and securitized assets as part of the income sleeve. These markets can provide a meaningful yield [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mpickup over government bonds with strong structural protections and diversification benefits, particularly when investors can be selective about structure and [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcollateral quality.[0m                                                                                                                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBloomberg, as of 12/05/2025. bp = basis point.[0m                                                                                                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mEmerging-Market Debt as a Diversifier[0m                                                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWe also see a role for emerging-market debt as an effective diversifier. Many emerging economies are on different fiscal and demographic paths than the U.S., and in[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32msome cases have been ahead of developed markets in lowering rates and fiscal deficits.[0m                                                                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSelect local-currency and hard-currency positions can help diversify the fiscal and rate risk embedded in a purely U.S.-centric bond portfolio, particularly when [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mstarting yields are attractive.[0m                                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mJP Morgan and Bloomberg, as of 12/05/2025. EMBI = JP Morgan Emerging Markets Bond Index, CEMBI = JP Morgan Corporate Emerging Markets Bond Index.[0m                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mEquities on the Right Side of AI[0m                                                                                                                                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mOn the equity side, the key question for us is who is on the right side of the AI cost revolution. A narrow group of large, AI-exposed companies has led markets for[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mgood reason, but dispersion is widening.[0m                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWe focus on businesses with durable profitability, strong balance sheets, and credible paths to using AI to improve margins or deepen competitive moats—not [0m          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcompanies that are adding buzzwords to their earnings calls. In a world where the rising tide will lift fewer boats, that quality and proof of concept filter [0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mmatters far more than it did a few years ago.[0m                                                                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBloomberg, as of 12/03/2025[0m                                                                                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFrom Gambling to Investing[0m                                                                                                                                            [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIf there’s a single message we’d leave you with as we head toward 2026, it’s this: the game has changed.[0m                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe period when nearly every chip you placed in the market seemed to pay off is behind us. Inflation is no longer the central issue. Labor dynamics and the [0m          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mdistributional effects of AI now matter more, and policy is likely to move from overtly restrictive toward something closer to neutral. Growth can remain resilient,[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mbut dispersion and default risk are rising.[0m                                                                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThat’s an environment where gambling in markets offers much worse odds than it did a few years ago. Investing, by contrast, still offers very good odds: owning [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mdurable income, strong balance sheets, and businesses on the right side of the productivity revolution, and then giving those positions time to compound. In our [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mview, the opportunity now belongs to those willing to act like investors, not gamblers — leaning into selectivity, patience, and discipline in a market that is once[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32magain distinguishing between quality and everything else.[0m                                                                                                             [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mThe Morningstar Rating for funds, or "star rating", is calculated for managed products (including mutual funds, variable annuity and variable life subaccounts, [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mexchange-traded funds, closed-end funds, and separate accounts) with at least a three-year history. Exchange-traded funds and open-ended mutual funds are considered[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32ma single population for comparative purposes. It is calculated based on a Morningstar Risk-Adjusted Return measure (excluding any applicable sales charges) that [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32maccounts for variation in a managed product's monthly excess performance, placing more emphasis on downward variations and rewarding consistent performance. The top[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m10% of products in each product category receive 5 stars, the next 22.5% receive 4 stars, the next 35% receive 3 stars, the next 22.5% receive 2 stars, and the [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mbottom 10% receive 1 star. The Overall Morningstar Rating for a managed product is derived from a weighted average of the performance figures associated with its [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthree-, five-, and 10-year 60-119 months of total returns, and 50% 10-year rating/30% five-year rating/20% three-year rating for 120 or more months of total [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mreturns. While the 10-year overall star rating formula seems to give the most weight to the 10-year period, the most recent three-year period actually has the [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mgreatest impact because it is included in all three rating periods.[0m                                                                                                   [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mFixed income risks include interest-rate and credit risk. Typically, when interest rates rise, there is a corresponding decline in bond values. Credit risk refers [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mto the possibility that the bond issuer will not be able to make principal and interest payments. International investing involves risks, including risks related to[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mforeign currency, limited liquidity, less government regulation, and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic, or other [0m          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mdevelopments. These risks may be heightened for investments in emerging markets. International investing involves risks, including risks related to foreign [0m          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcurrency, limited liquidity, less government regulation and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThese risks may be heightened for investments in emerging markets.[0m                                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mStock and bond values fluctuate in price so the value of your investment can go down depending on market conditions. Non-investment-grade debt securities [0m            [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m(high-yield/junk bonds) may be subject to greater market fluctuations, risk of default or loss of income and principal than higher rated securities. Asset [0m           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mallocation strategies do not assure profit and do not protect against loss.[0m                                                                                           [32m│[0m

[32m│[0m  [32mMarket Signals and Shifts: What to watch in 2026[0m                                                                                                                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWhat's inside[0m                                                                                                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mPreface[0m                                                                                                                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mA view from State Street Associates[0m                                                                                                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mTroubling asset allocation precedents[0m                                                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIs US equity dominance coming to an end?[0m                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWhat AI and alternative data reveal about interest rate markets in 2026[0m                                                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mDollar bounce or more of the same?[0m                                                                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mRerating emerging markets: From cyclical to structural ownership[0m                                                                                                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mOur award-winning research team draws on State Street’s proprietary data to address pressing questions shaping global markets in 2026.[0m                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mPreface[0m                                                                                                                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mJanuary 2026[0m                                                                                                                                                          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mTony Bisegna [0m                                                                                                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mHead of State Street Markets[0m                                                                                                                                          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mOur second annual State Street Markets outlook, Market Signals and Shifts: What to watch in 2026 , examines the dominant forces shaping the year ahead through a [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mlens that challenges consensus thinking.[0m                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe outlook draws on the work of our award-winning research team and focuses on understanding investor behavior, regional risk dynamics and how signals evolve when [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mmarkets move away from historic norms. By combining State Street’s suite of proprietary indicators — including a novel prediction methodology — and applying new [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32martificial intelligence (AI) tools to our data, we’re bringing greater clarity to a fast-shifting market landscape that has become difficult to interpret with [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mtraditional frameworks alone. This approach is further explained by David Turkington, head of State Street Associates, in the accompanying video below.[0m               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAt its core, Market Signals and Shifts: What to watch in 2026 is designed to address the most consequential questions investors are grappling with this year. Rather[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthan a point forecast, these insights can reveal market shifts and momentum changes ahead of the broader narrative.[0m                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mRead our experts’ commentary to learn:[0m                                                                                                                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWhy institutional investor asset allocation precedents are troubling Whether US equities can continue their long run of outperformance What AI and alternative data [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcan tell us about interest rate markets Whether the US dollar can bounce back from its worst decline in almost a decade Why emerging markets should be reconsidered[0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAs the investment landscape grows more complex, our experienced research team remains focused on helping investors make sense of the signals and shifts across [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mglobal markets with an eye toward identifying new opportunities and preparing for volatility.[0m                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWe hope you find this outlook useful, and we look forward to providing you with periodic updates in the year ahead.[0m                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mShare[0m                                                                                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mA view from State Street Associates[0m                                                                                                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mDavid Turkington, head of State Street Associates, describes the unique approach, tools and analysis that have shaped this edition of the State Street Markets [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32moutlook.[0m                                                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mVideo content has been blocked in accordance with your cookie settings. You can access this feature by accepting all cookies or adjusting your cookie settings [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mbelow.[0m                                                                                                                                                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mCookie Settings[0m                                                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAllow All Cookies[0m                                                                                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mView the transcript[0m                                                                                                                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAt State Street Markets, our goal in research is to help investors stay a step ahead of key market trends.[0m                                                            [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWe've done this for so many years, looking at proprietary indicators of investor positioning and flow, as well as real time daily measures of inflation and market [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32msentiment.[0m                                                                                                                                                            [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBut one of the things we're really excited about these days is applying AI increasingly to get even faster trends and synthesize the information across all these [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mexciting data sources.[0m                                                                                                                                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFor example, we're working with our colleagues at MediaStats to apply large language models to central bank media tones to understand the likely direction and [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mdispersion in trends across where interest rates are potentially headed due to policy shifts.[0m                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWe're also applying a new and exciting prediction method called relevance based prediction to gauge where asset allocation trends might be headed and which assets [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcan perform going forward in 2026.[0m                                                                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFor example, what should we expect for equities based on the most relevant periods in history that we can identify?[0m                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAre those periods showing risk and downside potential or potential for a lot of gains?[0m                                                                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIn fact, it may be a bit of both, and the picture is murky, but this type of historical analysis gives us extra perspective on the range of risks and possibilities [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32min markets.[0m                                                                                                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWe're excited to follow these trends and more and try to stay one step ahead in 2026 by taking a different view on markets and data.[0m                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mTroubling asset allocation precedents[0m                                                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mMegan Czasonis, head of Portfolio Management Research, State Street Associates[0m                                                                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mMichael Metcalfe, head of Macro Strategy, State Street Markets[0m                                                                                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIn our 2025 edition of What to watch , we highlighted institutional investors’ overall portfolio allocation as a key issue for markets. This may be even more [0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mimportant in 2026 as investors continue to allocate a historically high percentage of their overall portfolios to equities, relative to bonds.[0m                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAccording to our indicators of institutional investor behavior, over the past 25 years the average allocation to equities has been about 20 percent above the [0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mallocation to fixed income. This is in line with the traditional portfolio theory of 60/40 equity/bond allocation. But as we begin 2026, that 20 percent [0m             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mover-allocation to equities is above 28 percent — a 15-year high reached this past October.[0m                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mSuch unusually inflated levels of optimism about equities, especially in the face of unprecedented macro policy uncertainty and a fundamental reorganization of the [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mglobal economy, have been largely vindicated over the past 12 months. One might assume that having passed the stress tests of 2025, confidence for 2026 should be [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32meven higher. Nevertheless, episodes of equity market volatility since peak optimism in October are a reminder that with such elevated levels of equity holdings [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcomes added market vulnerability.[0m                                                                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWhat do high equity allocations mean for returns in 2026?[0m                                                                                                             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mUsing our novel Relevance-Based Prediction (RBP) technology, 1 we can explore what typically happens to relative equity-bond returns following time periods with [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mhigh equity-to-bond portfolio allocations. By analyzing and extrapolating from the most relevant past experiences, RBP generates a future projection. In the [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mprocess, it can capture complex relationships in a transparent way.[0m                                                                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mTwo things are immediately striking about the top 20 percent of periods that were selected as most relevant to investors’ current equity overweight (see Figure 1). [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFirst, there is no recent precedent (in the past decade). Second, the existing precedents are clustered between July 1999 and May 2001, at the peak of the dot-com [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mbubble, and then again between August 2004 and June 2008, during the housing bubble and the Great Financial Crisis.[0m                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAn important takeaway from these relevant periods is that they include both the build-up to the crises, when equity returns were strong, and the eventual market [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcrashes themselves. This is reflected in the range of equity-bond return predictions generated by RBP from each month in these relevant time periods. These [0m          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mindividual or “solo” predictions represent each month’s “vote” for how equities will perform in 2026. An average of all these forecasts suggests that equities may [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32munderperform bonds by 3.2 percent in the coming 12 months (see Figure 2). Interestingly, the solo predictions are split between very negative and very positive [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mvotes, indicating a high degree of uncertainty.[0m                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mDon’t expect business as usual in 2026[0m                                                                                                                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAt first glance, this looks like a rather unusual way to begin a year-ahead outlook. The bimodal outcome looks to be the statistical equivalent of the famed [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“two-handed economist.” And we note that it is very different from consensus expectations, which largely forecast an average year for equity and fixed-income [0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mreturns.[0m                                                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBut it is in keeping with typical market outcomes during a calendar year, when mean returns are rarely observed and outliers regularly feature. In a recent episode [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mof the Street Signals podcast, David Dredge, CIO of hedge fund Convex Strategies noted, “The annual returns of the Standard & Poor’s 500, going back to 1929 are 7.9[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mpercent. And the frequency of that average is three times…in 96 years. Three times. The standard deviation is 18.8 percent. What’s driving the returns? The mean or [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthe variance? Obviously, the variance, massively.”[0m                                                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThis view reinforces the main takeaway from our analysis: When allocations to equities are so high, what follows is never average. The implications are profound. In[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m2026, investors need to prepare for a range of outcomes and be ready to hedge accordingly, whether it be through country, sector, asset, foreign exchange (FX) tilts[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mor by employing multi-asset and alternative strategies to boost portfolio resilience . We’ll explore some of these options in the following articles covering the [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32moutlook for equities, fixed income, currencies and emerging markets.[0m                                                                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIs US equity dominance coming to an end?[0m                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mMarija Veitmane, head of Equity Research, State Street Markets[0m                                                                                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFor years, United States stocks have been favored by global investors. This preference was based on a simple idea: In a world of sluggish economic and earnings [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mgrowth, markets reward stocks with the best earnings, and the US has had the most profitable companies (see Figure 3). Hence, it is no surprise that US stocks have [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mled equity markets for over a decade now. However, this outperformance has pushed valuation and positioning risks to high levels, indicating that earnings dominance[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mis critical for US stocks to continue to outperform in 2026.[0m                                                                                                          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mConsensus forecasts support continued US earnings dominance in 2026 [0m                                                                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mCurrent analyst consensus expectations support the case for US stocks to continue to lead the rest of the world. Analysts expect US stocks to grow earnings by 13.5 [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mpercent in 2026, ahead of just 8.7 percent for Europe, Australasia and the Far East (EAFE). Recent earnings revisions in the US are also dominated by upgrades while[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthe outlook for rest of the world is getting weaker.[0m                                                                                                                  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mHowever, caution is still required as the majority of earnings growth in the US is expected to come from a small group of large stocks — the “US Magnificent 7” (see[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFigure 4). Indeed, earnings growth in the US looks a lot less spectacular when we strip out these top seven technology companies, particularly against emerging [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mmarkets (EM) (see our analysis of EM later in this outlook). Despite that, earnings expectations for US equities still outshine most regional rivals.[0m                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAnother important insight for 2026 earnings is that analysts are yet again expecting earnings growth to broaden in other sectors in the US and other regions in the [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mworld, potentially catching up to the US Magnificent 7. These broadening earnings expectations have been a consistent theme in analyst forecasts since the 2022 [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mpost-COVID-19 recovery, yet they have failed to materialize.[0m                                                                                                          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mOf course, past performance does not guarantee future returns and there is no reason for past earnings disappointments to carry into 2026, so we would recommend [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mwatching the trend in relative earnings growth closely. Historically, the best predictors of future earnings have been capital expenditure and operating leverage. [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFor now, both of those drivers remain heavily skewed toward the US, and especially toward technology companies.[0m                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mInstitutional investors remain overweight in US equities despite brief interactions with other regions[0m                                                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAccording to our Institutional Investor Indicators, investors are maintaining a healthy degree of skepticism about potential earnings growth outside the US. Our [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32manalysis of investor behavior trends in 2025, alongside developments in earnings expectations, may shed more light on investment plans in 2026 (see Figure 5).[0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAt the start of the year, institutional investors questioned US exceptionalism as tariffs were seen as a supply shock (potentially raising prices and slowing [0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32meconomic growth) making it hard for the Federal Reserve to reignite the economy if needed. As a result, they shifted to some degree from the US to Europe, where the[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mpotential for a fiscal boost supported earnings growth expectations. However, a European earnings recovery has failed to materialize (currently EPS growth is [0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mexpected to end the year at -2 percent) and institutional investors abandoned European stocks.[0m                                                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mLater in the year, investors were encouraged by the Chinese authorities’ efforts to boost domestic demand and they reduced their underweight positions there. Yet [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32monce again, earnings growth failed to materialize (currently also tracking at -2 percent year-on-year) and institutional investors increasingly lost interest. [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mInstead, we now see investor appetite for Latin America and tech-heavy Asian stock markets increasing, where earnings expectations remain solid.[0m                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mFinally, recent elections in Japan have rekindled investor interest there with the hopes that a more expansionary Japanese fiscal policy and new structural reforms [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcould boost earnings growth. Yet here too, worries that inflation may strengthen the Japanese yen seem to be dampening recent enthusiasm.[0m                             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAfter having ventured into different markets this year, institutional investors have shown a preference for continuing to invest in what they perceive as reliable [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mearnings growth in the US. In fact, we have seen nearly six months of uninterrupted buying of US equities from institutional investors.[0m                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIn conclusion, we believe that the key factor to watch for with regard to regional equity allocation is relative earnings growth. For years, the profitability of US[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcompanies has towered over the rest of the world, which was rewarded by multi-year outperformance. Analysts, as is customary for their year-ahead analysis, are [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mlooking for earnings growth to broaden regionally. However, institutional investors, and we here at State Street Markets, are yet to be convinced.[0m                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWhat AI and alternative data reveal about interest rate markets in 2026[0m                                                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mMichael Guidi, head of Alternative Data, State Street Associates[0m                                                                                                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mMichael Metcalfe, head of Macro Strategy, State Street Markets[0m                                                                                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIn 2025, financial markets were shaped not just by the anticipation of AI-driven returns, but also by shifting expectations around central bank interest rate [0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mpolicy. These expectations led to significant moves in both short- and long-term interest rates, with ripple effects across asset classes. As we enter 2026, a key [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mquestion is: What can AI and alternative data — especially tools like State Street PriceStats — reveal about the likely divergence in interest rate markets? And [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32meven more critically, how might these insights help us anticipate the next move in short- and long-term rates?[0m                                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe power of language analytics to determine central bank tone[0m                                                                                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mCentral banks are prolific communicators, aiming to guide market expectations and smooth the impact of their policy decisions. The financial media amplifies these [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mmessages, often highlighting the most market-relevant statements. Large language models (LLMs) now enable us to systematically analyze this vast communication flow.[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIn partnership with fintech company MKT MediaStats, we can classify central bank statements and media coverage as “hawkish” (favoring tighter policy) or “dovish” [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m(favoring easier policy), and construct real-time indicators of monetary policy sentiment. In our experience, we have found that these AI-driven tone measures are [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mvaluable tools for forecasting policy shifts.[0m                                                                                                                         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAt the start of 2025, the European Central Bank (ECB) was the most dovish among the major central banks, but after its final rate cut in June, its tone shifted and [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mit is now the least dovish of the G3 (see Figure 6). Meanwhile, the Bank of Japan (BoJ) and the Federal Reserve have shown more variation in their tone. The BoJ, [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mafter years of ultra-accommodative policy, is now only slightly more dovish than the ECB, reflecting tentative normalization and a desire to avoid further yen [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mweakness.[0m                                                                                                                                                             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe Fed, by contrast, became markedly more dovish from June onwards and has since delivered rate cuts at the last three meetings of 2025. But we would be cautious [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mabout extrapolating from that. Our AI-based tone analysis also quantifies the degree of disagreement among individual central bankers, revealing the highest levels [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mof internal divergence in over four years at the Fed. This suggests a heightened risk of policy surprises and market volatility, and implies that the Fed will [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcontinue to stress a “data-dependent” approach.[0m                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mReal-time insights from alternative inflation data [0m                                                                                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe debate within the Fed’s chief policy-making body – the Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC) – will ultimately be shaped by the path of inflation. Despite [0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mvolatility, US inflation ended 2025 close to mid-year forecasts. State Street PriceStats, a high-frequency inflation tracker, has proven especially useful — [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcapturing downside surprises in official data in early 2025 and the summer’s inflation reacceleration, and even substituting for official data during the fourth [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mquarter US government shutdown.[0m                                                                                                                                       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAs 2026 begins, State Street PriceStats shows US annual inflation, particularly in goods, plateauing and starting to roll over in line with forecasts (see Figure [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m7). If the labor market continues to soften, this should reduce Fed disagreements and pave the way for further Fed easing, moving rates back toward neutral as the [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mconsensus expects.[0m                                                                                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWhat is perhaps an even more revealing finding from State Street PriceStats is the recent re-acceleration of inflation in Europe, and deceleration in Japan (albeit [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mfrom an elevated level). This divergence mirrors the shifts in central bank tone and points to a more fragmented global economic outlook. For Europe, it suggests [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthe next rate move could be upward, in line with the ECB’s more balanced tone. For Japan, again following the tone, it signals that any tightening cycle will likely[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mbe cautious and shallow.[0m                                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mA divergence in demand will drive bond markets in 2026[0m                                                                                                                [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe BoJ’s caution is rooted in nearly two decades of disinflation and a desire to avoid disruptions in long-term yields. Across asset classes, the message is again [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mone of divergence. Throughout 2025, asset manager demand for 30-year US Treasuries was below average for all but one month. Conversely, demand for Eurozone and [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mJapanese sovereign debt from international investors was above average.[0m                                                                                               [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIn the second half of 2025, more constructive price action in the US Treasury market suggests that other investor segments have stepped in to fill the gap. However,[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mfew sources of demand are as stable as long-term asset managers. If these buyers do not return in 2026 and the Fed continues to cut short-term rates, the risk of [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mgreater volatility in yield and yield curve steepness looks to be higher in the US than elsewhere.[0m                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mOverall in 2026, we expect an unusual divergence in G3 policy rates and continued risks at the long-end of the US curve.[0m                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mDollar bounce or more of the same?[0m                                                                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mLee Ferridge, head of Multi-Asset Strategy for the Americas, State Street Markets[0m                                                                                     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m2025 was a tough year for the US dollar (USD). The US Dollar Index (DXY) has fallen by 9.4 percent so far this year; its worst annual performance since 2016 and the[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32msecond worst since 2003. You might expect that what goes down must come up — but is that always true?[0m                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mOne of the most notable takeaways from our US Dollar Investor Behavior Indicators is that, despite the USD’s poor performance in 2025, overseas owners of US [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mfinancial assets did not materially increase their USD hedge ratios this year (see Figure 8). That means they maintained their exposure to the USD in the face of [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mits decline.[0m                                                                                                                                                          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAs Figure 8 illustrates, rather than overseas investors increasing their USD hedge ratios in 2025, they have actually reduced them slightly, from close to 58 [0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mpercent at the start of the year, to 56 percent now. At its nadir in 2025, the overseas USD hedge ratio fell to just under 53 percent; its lowest level since 2016.[0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWhen it comes to FX hedging activity, it was US domestic investors who had the most pronounced reaction in the lead-up to, and immediately following, the [0m            [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m“Liberation Day” market volatility. US investors more than halved the hedge ratio on their foreign currency exposures (i.e., they sold USD) from around 25 percent [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mat the start of 2025, to a low of a little above 12 percent. The current reading is just over 13 percent. In large part, US investors were behind the dramatic [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32msell-off in the USD seen in the first half of this year.[0m                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe next question to consider then is: Given the USD’s poor performance, why did foreign investors hold firm? Why didn’t they increase their USD hedge ratios, [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthereby reducing their exposure to a declining USD?[0m                                                                                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe answer is captured in Figure 9. As our chart illustrates, the decision over how much to hedge USD exposure is heavily influenced by the cost of the hedge. In [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mearly 2022, before the FOMC started its post-COVID-19 hiking cycle, it was effectively free for overseas investors to hedge their USD risk. At that time, our USD [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mForeign Hedge Ratio Indicator was at 78 percent. Since then, it has steadily declined due to rising hedging costs for overseas investors converting to USD. Although[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthe US interest rate premium over a DXY-weighted G10 index has narrowed in recent weeks, foreign investors will still, on average, be giving up around 1.5 percent [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mof their returns to hedge the USD.[0m                                                                                                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mHowever, when we look ahead to 2026, consensus expectations suggest that this hedging cost will fall meaningfully over the coming quarters. The FOMC is currently [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mpriced to cut a further two times in 2026. While not particularly significant, it stands in sharp contrast to the interest rate expectations across much of the G10,[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mas highlighted earlier in this 2026 outlook (see Figure 3).[0m                                                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mAs Figure 10 illustrates, the FOMC is expected to ease by more than any other G10 central bank in 2026. Additionally, five of the G10 central banks are currently [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mexpected to raise rates next year. If these consensus expectations are realized, 2026 would represent a very unusual year of divergence in G10 central bank policy. [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe last time three or more G10 central banks diverged in policy was in 2004. Indeed, since 2019, only the BoJ has moved counter to the majority. Perhaps even more [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32msignificantly, the Fed is poised to cut rates next year (along with the Bank of England and Norges Bank).[0m                                                             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWhether others will hike while the Fed is easing remains to be seen. However, if market pricing proves accurate, 2026 could be an interesting FX year for the USD. [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIn 2004, when the Fed was cutting rates while more than three other G10 central banks were raising rates, the DXY fell by 7 percent, having fallen by 15 percent the[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mprevious year.[0m                                                                                                                                                        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mIn answer to the question posed at the start, what goes down does not necessarily have to go up the following year. With overseas USD hedge ratios at historically [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mlow levels and the cost of hedging USD exposure set to fall in 2026, a USD bounce in 2026 seems unlikely.[0m                                                             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mRerating emerging markets: From cyclical to structural ownership[0m                                                                                                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mBen Luk, senior multi-asset strategist, State Street Markets[0m                                                                                                          [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mEmerging markets had a stellar year in 2025, with double-digit gains across carry, bonds and equities. This rally was due to a combination of external and internal [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mtailwinds, which led to better inflows across the region. A weak USD backdrop, coupled with a significant decrease in cross-asset volatility, encouraged investors [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32min EM, while macro conditions surprised on the upside, thanks to strong demand for AI-related products despite persistent tariff threats. Last but not least, [0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcredible central bank policy and prudent government spending underpinned confidence in EMs.[0m                                                                           [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mYet surprisingly, as we begin 2026, according to our Institutional Investor Indicators (see Figure 11), institutional investors have remained on the sidelines with [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32munderweight positions across EM FX, local currency sovereign bonds and equities. This makes the region an attractive opportunity for investors seeking to diversify [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32maway from overweight positions in both Europe and the US.[0m                                                                                                             [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mUnlike their developed market (DM) counterparts, EM central banks were not aggressive in easing policies over the past 12 months as they managed to build hefty FX [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mreserves during a weak USD environment to potentially offset capital outflows. According to State Street PriceStats, aggregate EM online prices have fallen 5.2 [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mpercent year-on-year — the lowest reading in the past 15 years —whereas DM inflation is back to a three-year high (see Figure 12). This divergence in price dynamics[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mhas forced DM central banks to keep rates anchored, whereas EM central banks can still cut rates in the medium term due to lower underlying inflation dynamics — [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32malthough markets have priced in a lot of easing going into 2026. Moreover, US Treasury movements this year have been driven not only by cyclical factors, but also [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mby structural concerns such as debt sustainability, central bank independence and the future trajectory of Fed Chairman Powell; still, EM bonds have demonstrated [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mresiliency in 2025.[0m                                                                                                                                                   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWe anticipate EM yield differentials to compress further in 2026. However, active management will be essential to determine which countries and which parts of the [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcurve to invest in, as idiosyncratic factors such as elections, defaults or renewed geopolitical tensions may arise — even if EM central banks collectively look to [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mextend their easing cycles. Investors should consider this asset class not as a cyclical trade, but as a long-term strategic allocation, as foreign ownership [0m        [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mremains well below long-term average.[0m                                                                                                                                 [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mMonetary policy serves as a foundation for the stability of EMs, but what matters in the long run is economic growth and trade. World trade has continued to [0m         [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mstrengthen despite tariff threats, while economic surprises in EM have turned positive since the start of the fourth quarter 2025. In 2026, gross domestic product [0m   [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m(GDP) growth in EM is expected to remain unchanged at 4.1 percent, similar to growth levels seen in 2025 and 2024.[0m                                                    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mWe expect EM exports to remain resilient in 2026 given the ongoing surge in AI and chip innovation, as China, South Korea and Taiwan are the major beneficiaries of [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mthe global technology cycle. However, China’s growth will likely remain under pressure due to geopolitical tensions with the US (and most recently, Japan), but this[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcould force Beijing to be proactive instead of reactive. Policymakers are focused on policies to fight against deflation, and Beijing should encourage consumer [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mspending, self-sustainability and high-value-chain manufacturing, as well as provide indirect support to the property and labor markets. We see the potential for [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcontinued positive equity performance in 2026, as corporate earnings improve due to Asian tech proxies while investors take advantage of the deep valuation discount[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mrelative to US equities.[0m                                                                                                                                              [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mThe biggest concern for EM has, and will always be, renewed USD strength — although not all EM assets are as vulnerable. As we track real money flows over time, we [0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mhave observed a rotation in investor preference of EM equities over bonds in a rising dollar environment, but not a diversion from the entire region (see Figure [0m     [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32m13). EM bond managers are more sensitive to USD strength, as gains in FX would be wiped out and spreads would widen while hedging costs would increase. On the [0m       [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mcontrary, weaker EM currencies can still benefit from equities if this translates into stronger exports and greater market share — ultimately leading to higher [0m      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mearnings growth.[0m                                                                                                                                                      [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mInvestors have aggressively sold EM in the past, given a downturn in growth and a lack of accountability from central banks and the government — but the EM of today[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mare not the same as in the past. Credible policy management and improving fiscal balances, coupled with steady macro conditions, indicate that EM as a whole deserve[0m  [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mmore attention, especially for investors looking to expand their risk spectrum and increase their diversification. As we enter 2026, EM as an asset class remains [0m    [32m│[0m
[32m│[0m  [32mextremely under-owned and undervalued.[0m                                                                                                                                [32m│[0m
